Check out the latest report covering our evaluations on query pipeline performance, and the product improvements made based on these learnings.\n&amp;nbsp;\nA common practice for implementing the retrieval step in retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) applications is to use vector search. This approach finds relevant passages using semantic similarity. We fully support this pattern in&amp;nbsp;Azure AI Search&amp;nbsp;(formerly Azure Cognitive Search) and offer additional capabilities that complement and build on vector search to deliver markedly improved relevance.\n&amp;nbsp;\nIn this blog post, we share the results of experiments conducted on Azure AI Search and present a quantitative basis to support the use of hybrid retrieval + semantic ranking as the most effective approach for improved relevance out-of–the-box. This is especially true for Generative AI scenarios where applications use the RAG pattern, though these conclusions apply to many general search use cases as well.\n&amp;nbsp;\n1. The Technology Behind Azure AI Search\nThe query stack in Azure AI Search follows a pattern that’s often used in sophisticated search systems, where there are two main layers of execution: retrieval and ranking.&amp;nbsp;\n&amp;nbsp;\nRetrieval – Often called L1, the goal of this step is to quickly find all the documents from the index that satisfy the search criteria -possibly across millions or billions of documents. These are scored to pick the top few (typically in the order of 50) to return to the user or to feed to the next layer. Azure AI Search supports (3) different L1 modes:\n\nKeyword: Uses traditional full-text search methods – content is broken into terms through language-specific text analysis, inverted indexes are created for fast retrieval, and the BM25 probabilistic model is used for scoring.\nVector: Documents are converted from text to vector representations using an embedding model. Retrieval is performed by generating a query embedding and finding the documents whose vectors are closest to the query’s.&amp;nbsp; We used Azure Open AI text-embedding-ada-002 (Ada-002) embeddings and cosine similarity for all our tests in this post.\nHybrid: Performs both keyword and vector retrieval and applies a fusion step to select the best results from each technique. Azure AI Search currently uses Reciprocal Rank Fusion (RRF) to produce a single result set.\n\n&amp;nbsp;\nRanking – also called L2, takes a subset of the top L1 results and computes higher quality relevance scores to reorder the result set. The L2 can improve the L1&apos;s ranking because it applies more computational power to each result. The L2 ranker can only reorder what the L1 already found – if the L1 missed an ideal document, the L2 can&apos;t fix that. L2 ranking is critical for RAG applications to make sure the best results are in the top positions.\n\nSemantic ranking is performed by Azure AI Search&apos;s L2 ranker which utilizes multi-lingual, deep learning models adapted from Microsoft Bing. The Semantic ranker can rank the top 50 results from the L1.\n\n&amp;nbsp;\n2. Hybrid Retrieval + Semantic Ranking yields the best grounding results for Generative AI Applications\nGenerative AI applications need to be grounded by content retrieved from indexes that contain the knowledge necessary for relevant responses. If irrelevant content is passed to the LLM, it works counter to this objective, and requires the model to filter extraneous information.&amp;nbsp; This can reduce the quality of generated responses and increase latency and operating costs.\n&amp;nbsp;\nWe performed tests on representative customer indexes as well as popular academic benchmarks to test the quality of content retrieval results. Across the board, the most effective retrieval engine for most scenarios is achieved by:\n\nchunking long form content,\nusing hybrid retrieval (combining keyword and vector search), and\nenabling semantic ranking&amp;nbsp;\n\n\n\n\n\n\nSearch Configuration\n&amp;nbsp;\n&amp;nbsp;\n\n\nCustomer datasets\n&amp;nbsp;\n[NDCG@3]\n\n\nBeir\n&amp;nbsp;\n[NDCG@10]\n\n\nMultilingual Academic (MIRACL) [NDCG@10]\n\n\n\n\nKeyword\n\n\n40.6\n\n\n40.6\n\n\n49.6\n\n\n\n\nVector (Ada-002)\n\n\n43.8\n\n\n45.0\n\n\n58.3\n\n\n\n\nHybrid (Keyword + Vector)\n\n\n48.4\n\n\n48.4\n\n\n58.8\n\n\n\n\nHybrid + Semantic ranker\n\n\n60.1\n\n\n50.0\n\n\n72.0\n\n\n\n\n\nTable 1: Retrieval comparison using Azure AI Search in various retrieval modes on customer and academic benchmarks. See §6.1 How we generated the numbers in this post and §6.2 Search and Dataset configuration for Table 1 for the setup and measurement details.&amp;nbsp;&amp;nbsp;\n&amp;nbsp;\n3. Hybrid Retrieval brings out the best of Keyword and Vector Search\nKeyword and vector retrieval tackle search from different perspectives, which yield complementary capabilities. Vector retrieval semantically matches queries to passages with similar meanings. This is powerful because embeddings are less sensitive to misspellings, synonyms, and phrasing differences and can even work in cross lingual scenarios. Keyword search is useful because it prioritizes matching specific, important words that might be diluted in an embedding.\n&amp;nbsp;\nUser search can take many forms. Hybrid retrieval consistently brings out the best from both retrieval methods across query types. With the most effective L1, the L2 ranking step can significantly improve the quality of results in the top positions.\n&amp;nbsp;\n\n\n\n\n\nQuery type\n&amp;nbsp;\n&amp;nbsp;\n\n\nKeyword\n&amp;nbsp;\n[NDCG@3]\n\n\nVector\n&amp;nbsp;\n[NDCG@3]\n\n\nHybrid\n&amp;nbsp;\n[NDCG@3]\n\n\nHybrid + Semantic ranker [NDCG@3]\n\n\n\n\nConcept seeking queries\n\n\n39.0\n\n\n45.8\n\n\n46.3\n\n\n59.6\n\n\n\n\nFact seeking queries\n\n\n37.8\n\n\n49.0\n\n\n49.1\n\n\n63.4\n\n\n\n\nExact snippet search\n\n\n51.1\n\n\n41.5\n\n\n51.0\n\n\n60.8\n\n\n\n\nWeb search-like queries\n\n\n41.8\n\n\n46.3\n\n\n50.0\n\n\n58.9\n\n\n\n\nKeyword queries\n\n\n79.2\n\n\n11.7\n\n\n61.0\n\n\n66.9\n\n\n\n\nLow query/doc term overlap\n\n\n23.0\n\n\n36.1\n\n\n35.9\n\n\n49.1\n\n\n\n\nQueries with misspellings\n\n\n28.8\n\n\n39.1\n\n\n40.6\n\n\n54.6\n\n\n\n\nLong queries\n\n\n42.7\n\n\n41.6\n\n\n48.1\n\n\n59.4\n\n\n\n\nMedium queries\n\n\n38.1\n\n\n44.7\n\n\n46.7\n\n\n59.9\n\n\n\n\nShort queries\n\n\n53.1\n\n\n38.8\n\n\n53.0\n\n\n63.9\n\n\n\n\n\nTable 2: NDCG@3 comparison across query types and search configurations. See §6.3 Query Type definitions for Table 2 for a more detailed description of each query type. All vector retrieval modes used the same document chunks (512 token chunks w/25% overlap with Ada-002 embedding model over customer query/document benchmark). Sentence boundaries were preserved in all cases.\n&amp;nbsp;\n4. Your Document Chunking strategy matters\nChunking solves 3 problems for Generative AI applications:&amp;nbsp;\n\nSplitting long documents into limited-length passages allows multiple retrieved documents to be passed to the LLM within its context window limit.\nChunking provides a mechanism for the most relevant passages of a given document to be ranked first.\nVector search has a per-model limit to how much content can be embedded into each vector.\n\n&amp;nbsp;\nEmbedding each chunk into its own vector keeps the input within the embedding model’s token limit and enables the entire document to be searchable in an ANN search index without truncation. Most deep embedding models have a limit of 512 tokens. Ada-002 has a limit of 8,192 tokens. Moderate length documents can have tens of thousands of tokens. The benefit of chunking is particularly strong when the documents are very long or the answers to queries are found later in the document:\n&amp;nbsp;\n\n\n\n\n\nRetrieval Configuration\n&amp;nbsp;\n&amp;nbsp;\n\n\nSingle vector per document\n&amp;nbsp;[Recall@50]\n\n\nChunked documents\n&amp;nbsp;[Recall@50]\n\n\n\n\nQueries whose answer is in long documents\n\n\n28.2\n\n\n45.7 \n\n\n\n\nQueries whose answer is deep into a document\n\n\n28.7\n\n\n51.4 \n\n\n\n\n\nTable 3: Recall@50 comparison using (1) a single vector to represent each document (first 4096 tokens of each document were vectorized and the rest were truncated) vs (2) chunking each document into 512 token chunks w/25% overlap with Ada-002 embedding model over customer query/document benchmark. Sentence boundaries were preserved in all cases. Metric computed with vector retrieval only (no Semantic ranking).&amp;nbsp;\n&amp;nbsp;\nAnother key consideration is that embedding models must compress all the semantic content of a passage into a limited number of floating-point numbers (e.g. Ada-002 uses 1,536 dimensions). If developers encode a long passage with multiple topics into a single vector, important nuance can get lost. Our analysis shows that using large chunks reduces retrieval performance.\n&amp;nbsp;\n\n\n\n\n\nRetrieval Configuration \n\n\nRecall@50\n\n\n\n\n512 input tokens per vector\n\n\n42.4\n\n\n\n\n1024 input tokens per vector\n\n\n37.5\n\n\n\n\n4096 input tokens per vector\n\n\n36.4\n\n\n\n\n8191 input tokens per vector\n\n\n34.9\n\n\n\n\n\nTable 4: Recall@50 comparison of different chunk sizes with Ada-002 embedding model over customer query/document benchmark. Sentence boundaries were preserved in all cases. Metric computed with vector retrieval only (no Semantic ranking).\n&amp;nbsp;\nThere are many ways developers can build the input for each vector. For example, they can overlap each chunk so there is shared context between them, or they can add in the document title or key topics into each vector to give more context. A strategy of terminating vectors at natural sentence and paragraph breaks is both simple and effective.&amp;nbsp;\n&amp;nbsp;\n\n\n\n\n\nChunk boundary strategy\n\n\nRecall@50\n\n\n\n\n512 tokens, break at token boundary\n\n\n40.9\n\n\n\n\n512 tokens, preserve sentence boundaries\n\n\n42.4\n\n\n\n\n512 tokens with 10% overlapping chunks\n\n\n43.1\n\n\n\n\n512 tokens with 25% overlapping chunks\n\n\n43.9\n\n\n\n\n\nTable 5: Recall@50 comparison of different chunk boundary strategies using 512 tokens with Ada002 embedding model over customer query/document benchmark. Metric computed with vector retrieval only (no semantic ranking).\n&amp;nbsp;\n5. Semantic Ranking Puts the Best Results at the Top\nGenerative AI scenarios typically use the top 3 to 5 results as their grounding context to prioritize the most important results. AI Search applications work best with a calibrated relevance score that can be used to filter out low quality results.&amp;nbsp;\n&amp;nbsp;\nThe semantic ranker runs the query and documents text simultaneously though transformer models that utilize the cross-attention mechanism to produce a ranker score. The query and document chunk score is calibrated to a range that is consistent across all indexes and queries. A score of 0 represents a very irrelevant chunk, and a score of 4 represents an excellent one. In the chart below, Hybrid + Semantic ranking finds the best content for the LLM at each result set size.&amp;nbsp;\n\nChart 1: Percentage of queries where high-quality chunks are found in the top 1 to 5 results, compared across search configurations. All retrieval modes used the same set of customer query/document benchmark. Document chunks were 512 tokens with 25% overlap.&amp;nbsp; Vector and hybrid retrieval used Ada-002 embeddings.&amp;nbsp;\n&amp;nbsp;\nIn conclusion, the results of the above experiments on real-world and benchmark datasets lead us to recommend the combined strategies of chunked content, hybrid search, and semantic ranking. To test these findings against your users’ questions and datasets, please try the resources linked below to get started:\n\nAzure AI Search documentation | Microsoft Learn\nSemantic ranker (formerly semantic search) - Azure AI Search | Microsoft Learn\n\n&amp;nbsp;\n6. Appendix – How we ran these tests\n&amp;nbsp;\n6.1 How we generated the numbers in this post\nTo assess which retrieval systems and configurations performed the best, we followed best practices to generate comparable metrics. The high-level process was to replay a list of queries against several document indexes for each configuration and produce scores of how good the retrieval and ranking was.&amp;nbsp;\n\nDocuments - We use a consistent set of documents sourced from either Azure customers (with their permission) or publicly available benchmarks.&amp;nbsp;\nQueries – We used a combination of end user queries and/or queries generated by several different GPT prompts using random snippets from the document index as grounding.\nScoring – We used benchmark-provided labels (and the official scoring library) for BEIR and other datasets. For customer datasets, we use a GPT prompt that was vetted against a library of high quality (internally reviewed) human ground-truth labels.\n\n&amp;nbsp;\nWe used 3 metrics to determine our recommendations:\n\nNDCG@10 – NDCG is a common information retrieval metric that provides a score between 0 and 100 based on how well a retrieval system (1) found the best results and (2) put those results in the ideal order (i.e. a sorted list from the best document to the worst) for all the queries in a given query set. The @10 means that the top 10 documents were considered in the score calculation. We used this metric and the pool of available labels for public benchmarks to be consistent with previous runs. Normalized Discounted Cumulative Gain (NDCG)&amp;nbsp;\nNDCG@3 – The same NDCG metric but computed on the top 3 documents. We use @3 because we aim to get the most accurate results in the top (3) for generative AI scenarios. We score the top 50 documents because Azure AI Search’s semantic ranker works on the top 50 results.\nRecall@50 – We count the number of documents that our scoring prompt rates as high quality within the top 50 retrieved results and divide it by the number of known good documents for that query.\n\n6.2 Search and Dataset configuration for Table 1\nSearch Configuration\nFor this table of results, all documents were broken into 512 token chunks w/25% overlap.\n\nKeyword: The full set of chunks were indexed as if each chunk was a full document. Searches were performed as usual with the keyword-based index (BM25 similarity) and we labeled the top 50.\nVector: All the chunks were embedded using Ada-002 and an ANN index was built. Each query was also embedded with Ada-002 and searched using cosine similarity. The top 50 were labeled.\nHybrid: The keyword index and vector index of the chunks were searched (taking the top 50 from each) and then the results were fused together using RRF. The top 50 documents (chunks) from RRF were labeled.\nHybrid + semantic ranking: Queries were performed against the hybrid search configuration with semantic ranking enabled.\n\n&amp;nbsp;\nDataset details\n\nCustomer datasets – retrieval benchmarks built from 4 different customer datasets spanned industries and document structures. All documents were imported from raw inputs (e.g. pptx, pdf, html) using Azure AI Search’s document ingestion pipeline. Queries for each dataset are a mixture of provided and GPT-generated queries. For ANN vector retrieval tests, all documents were chunked into 512 tokens with 25% overlap and embedded using Ada-002.\nBEIR – Imported the following datasets from the official source (beir-cellar/beir: A Heterogeneous Benchmark for Information Retrieval. Easy to use, evaluate your models across 15+ diverse IR datasets. (github.com)) and used the official labels and official scoring library to generate metrics. For ANN vector retrieval, each document was indexed using Ada-002 (in 99.9% of cases 1 vector was sufficient to represent the full document). Beir datasets used: nq, scidocs, touche2020, arguana, climate-fever, dbpedia, fever, hotpotqa, covid.&amp;nbsp;\nMiracl – Imported directly from the official source (project-miracl/miracl: A large-scale multilingual dataset for Information Retrieval. Thorough human-annotations across 18 diverse languages. (github.com)) and used official labels for scoring. Due to the size of the dataset the following simplifications were used: we sampled 250 queries per language and only documents with labels were vectorized and a combined multi-lingual index was built. The reported metric is the average across ar, bn, de, en, es, fa, fi, fr, hi, id, ja, ko, ru, sw, te, th, yo, zh languages.\n\n&amp;nbsp;\n6.3 Query Type definitions for Table 2\n\n\n\n\n\nQuery type\n\n\nExplanation\n\n\nExample\n\n\n\n\nConcept seeking queries\n\n\nAbstract questions that require multiple sentences to answer\n\n\n“Why should I use semantic search to rank results?”\n\n\n\n\nExact snippet search\n\n\nLonger queries that are exact sub-strings from the original paragraph\n\n\n“enables you to maximize the quality and value of your LLM investments most efficiently by feeding only relevant information”\n\n\n\n\nWeb search-like queries\n\n\nShortened queries similar to those commonly entered into a search engine\n\n\n“Best retrieval concept queries”\n\n\n\n\nLow query/doc term overlap\n\n\nQueries where the answer uses different words and phrases from the question [which can be challenging for a retrieval engine to find]\n\n\n“greatest technology for sorting” searching for a document that says: “Azure AI Search has the best models for ranking your content”\n\n\n\n\nFact seeking queries\n\n\nQueries with a single, clear answer\n\n\n“How many documents are semantically ranked”\n\n\n\n\nKeyword queries\n\n\nShort queries that consist of only the important identifier words.\n\n\n“semantic ranker”\n\n\n\n\nQueries with misspellings\n\n\nQueries with typos, transpositions and common misspellings introduced\n\n\n“Ho w mny documents are samantically r4nked”\n\n\n\n\nLong queries\n\n\nQueries longer than 20 tokens\n\n\n“This is a very long query that uses a lot of tokens in its composition and structure because it is verbose”\n\n\n\n\nMedium queries\n\n\nBetween 5 and 20 tokens long\n\n\n“This is a medium length query”\n\n\n\n\nShort queries\n\n\nQueries shorter than 5 tokens\n\n\n“Short query”.\n\n\n\n\n\n&amp;nbsp;\n&amp;nbsp;","mainEntityOfPage":{"@type":"WebPage","@id":"https://techcommunity.microsoft.com/blog/azure-ai-foundry-blog/azure-ai-search-outperforming-vector-search-with-hybrid-retrieval-and-reranking/3929167"},"headline":"Azure AI Search: Outperforming vector search with hybrid retrieval and alOverride":null,"page":{"id":"AllBlogs.Page","type":"CUSTOM","urlPath":"/blogs","__typename":"PageDescriptor"},"__typename":"PageResource"},{"lastUpdatedTime":1778174787259,"localOverride":null,"page":{"id":"TkbMessagePage","type":"TKB_ARTICLE","urlPath":"/kb/:boardId/:messageSubject/:messageId","__typename":"PageDescriptor"},"__typename":"PageResource"},{"lastUpdatedTime":1778174787259,"localOverride":null,"page":{"id":"BlogEditPage","type":"BLOG","urlPath":"/blog/:boardId/:messageSubject/:messageId/edit","__typename":"PageDescriptor"},"__typename":"PageResource"},{"lastUpdatedTime":1778174787259,"localOverride":null,"page":{"id":"ManageUsersPage","type":"USER","urlPath":"/users/manage/:tab?/:manageUsersTab?","__typename":"PageDescriptor"},"__typename":"PageResource"},{"lastUpdatedTime":1778174787259,"localOverride":null,"page":{"id":"ForumReplyPage","type":"FORUM_REPLY","urlPath":"/discussions/:boardId/:messageSubject/:messageId/replies/:replyId","__typename":"PageDescriptor"},"__typename":"PageResource"},{"lastUpdatedTime":1778174787259,"localOverride":null,"page":{"id":"PrivacyPolicyPage","type":"COMMUNITY","urlPath":"/privacypolicy","__typename":"PageDescriptor"},"__typename":"PageResource"},{"lastUpdatedTime":1778174787259,"localOverride":null,"page":{"id":"NotificationPage","type":"COMMUNITY","urlPath":"/notifications","__typename":"PageDescriptor"},"__typename":"PageResource"},{"lastUpdatedTime":1778174787259,"localOverride":null,"page":{"id":"UserPage","type":"USER","urlPath":"/users/:login/:userId","__typename":"PageDescriptor"},"__typename":"PageResource"},{"lastUpdatedTime":1778174787259,"localOverride":null,"page":{"id":"HealthCheckPage","type":"COMMUNITY","urlPath":"/health","__typename":"PageDescriptor"},"__typename":"PageResource"},{"lastUpdatedTime":1778174787259,"localOverride":null,"page":{"id":"OccasionReplyPage","type":"OCCASION_REPLY","urlPath":"/event/:boardId/:messageSubject/:messageId/comments/:replyId","__typename":"PageDescriptor"},"__typ reranking","id":"message:3929167","entityType":"BLOG_ARTICLE","eventPath":"category:azure-ai-foundry/category:products-services/category:communities/community:gxcuf89792board:azure-ai-foundry-blog/message:3929167","revisionNum":43,"repliesCount":6,"author":{"__ref":"User:user:2031971"},"depth":0,"hasGivenKudo":false,"board":{"__ref":"Blog:board:azure-ai-foundry-blog"},"conversation":{"__ref":"Conversation:conversation:3929167"},"messagePolicies":{"__typename":"MessagePolicies","canPublishArticleOnEdit":{"__typename":"PolicyResult","failureReason":{"__typename":"FailureReason","message":"error.lithium.policies.forums.policy_can_publish_on_edit_workflow_action.accessDenied","key":"error.lithium.policies.forums.policy_can_publish_on_edit_workflow_action.accessDenied","args":[]}},"canModerateSpamMessage":{"__typename":"PolicyResult","failureReason":{"__typename":"FailureReason","message":"error.lithium.policies.feature.moderation_spam.action.moderate_entity.allowed.accessDenied","key":"error.lithium.policies.feature.moderation_spam.action.moderate_entity.allowed.accessDenied","args":[]}},"canReply":{"__typename":"PolicyResult","failureReason":{"__typename":"FailureReason","message":"error.lithium.policies.forums.action.message.reply_to_entity.allow.accessDenied","key":"error.lithium.policies.forums.action.message.reply_to_entity.allow.accessDenied","args":[]}},"canAcceptSolution":{"__typename":"PolicyResult","failureReason":{"__typename":"FailureReason","message":"error.lithium.policies.accepted_solutions.action_allow.message.mark_as_accepted_solution.accessDenied","key":"error.lithium.policies.accepted_solutions.action_allow.message.mark_as_accepted_solution.accessDenied","args":[]}},"canRejectSolution":{"__typename":"PolicyResult","failureReason":{"__typename":"FailureReason","message":"error.lithium.policies.accepted_solutions.action_allow.message.unmark_as_accepted_solution.accessDenied","key":"error.lithium.policies.accepted_solutions.action_allow.message.unmark_as_accepted_solution.accessDenied","args":[]}},"canTag":{"__typename":"PolicyResult","failureReason":{"__typename":"FailureReason","message":"error.lithium.policies.labels.action.labelableentity.set_labels.allow.accessDenied","key":"error.lithium.policies.labels.action.labelableentity.set_labels.allow.accessDenied","args":[]}},"canEdit":{"__typename":"PolicyResult","failureReason":{"__typename":"FailureReason","message":"error.lithium.policies.forums.action_allow.edit_message.accessDenied","key":"error.lithium.policies.forums.action_allow.edit_message.accessDenied","args":[]}},"canKudo":{"__typename":"PolicyResult","failureReason":{"__typename":"FailureReason","message":"error.lithium.policies.kudos.action.entity.give_kudos.allow.accessDenied","key":"error.lithium.policies.kudos.action.entity.give_kudos.allow.accessDenied","args":[]}},"canModerateEntity":{"__typename":"PolicyResult","failureReason":{"__typename":"FailureReason","message":"error.lithium.policies.moderation.action.moderatableentity.moderate_entity.allow.accessDenied","key":"error.lithium.policies.moderation.action.moderatableentity.moderate_entity.allow.accessDenied","args":[]}},"canDelete":{"__typename":"PolicyResult","failureReason":{"__typename":"FailureReason","message":"error.lithium.policies.forums.action.delete_message_view.delete_message.allow.accessDenied","key":"error.lithium.policies.forums.action.delete_message_view.delete_message.allow.accessDenied","args":[]}},"canMoveMessage":{"__typename":"PolicyResult","failureReason":{"__typename":"FailureReason","message":"error.lithium.policies.forums.action.message.move_message.allow.accessDenied","key":"error.lithium.policies.forums.action.message.move_message.allow.accessDenied","args":[]}},"canMarkMessageReadOnly":{"__typename":"PolicyResult","failureReason":{"__typename":"FailureReason","message":"error.lithium.policies.feature.forums.action.mark_message_readonly.allowed.accessDenied","key":"error.lithium.policies.feature.forums.action.mark_message_readonly.allowed.accessDenied","args":[]}},"canFreezeTkbArticle":{"__typename":"PolicyResult","failureReason":{"__typename":"FailureReason","message":"error.lithium.policies.tkb.action_can_freeze_article.accessDenied","key":"error.lithium.policies.tkb.action_can_freeze_article.accessDenied","args":[]}},"canReportAbuse":{"__typename":"PolicyResult","failureReason":null},"canDeleteDraft":{"__typename":"PolicyResult","failureReason":{"__typename":"FailureReason","message":"error.lithium.policies.delete_drafts.accessDenied","key":"error.lithium.policies.delete_drafts.accessDenied","args":[]}}},"contentWorkflow":{"__typename":"ContentWorkflow","state":"PUBLISH","scheduledPublishTime":null,"scheduledTimezone":null,"userContext":{"__typename":"MessageWorkflowContext","canSubmitForReview":null,"canEdit":false,"canRecall":null,"canSubmitForPublication":null,"canReturnToAuthor":null,"canPublish":null,"canReturnToReview":null,"canSchedule":false},"shortScheduledTimezone":null},"readOnly":false,"editFrozen":false,"showMoveIndicator":false,"moderationData":{"__ref":"ModerationData:moderation_data:3929167"},"teaser":"\u003cspan class=\"lia-media-object lia-media-is-inline lia-media-size-medium\" data-image-alt=\"Hybrid retrieval with semantic ranking outperforms vector-only search\"\u003e\u003cbutton class=\"lia-media-unstyled-btn\" type=\"button\" aria-haspopup=\"true\" aria-label=\"Enlarge Image Hybrid retrieval with semantic ranking outperforms vector-only search\"\u003e\u003cimg src=\"https://techcommunity.microsoft.com/t5/s/gxcuf89792/images/bS0zOTI5MTY3LTUwODQ5Mmk2OTg4OTVERDY3NzEzOTUz?image-dimensions=400x223\u0026amp;revision=43\" width=\"400\" height=\"223\" alt=\"Hybrid retrieval with semantic ranking outperforms vector-only search\" /\u003e\u003c/button\u003e\u003c/span\u003e\n\u003cP\u003eHow do you find the best content to feed your generative AI models? In this blog post, we show you how to use Azure AI Search's hybrid retrieval and semantic ranking features to improve relevance for retrieval-augmented generation scenarios.\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/P\u003e","body":"\u003cP\u003e\u003cSPAN data-teams=\"true\"\u003e\u003cEM\u003eNote: Since this blog's publication, the Azure AI Search team has released new learnings on RAG quality. \u003c/EM\u003e\u003cA href=\"https://techcommunity.microsoft.com/blog/azure-ai-services-blog/raising-the-bar-for-rag-excellence-query-rewriting-and-new-semantic-ranker/4302729\" target=\"_blank\" aria-label=\"Link Check out the latest report\"\u003e\u003cEM\u003eCheck out the latest report\u003c/EM\u003e\u003c/A\u003e\u003cEM\u003e covering our evaluations on query pipeline performance, and the product improvements made based on these learnings.\u003c/EM\u003e\u003c/SPAN\u003e\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cP\u003eA common practice for implementing the retrieval step in retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) applications is to use vector search. This approach finds relevant passages using semantic similarity. We fully support this pattern in\u0026nbsp;\u003cA href=\"https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/products/ai-services/cognitive-search\" target=\"_self\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\"\u003eAzure AI Search\u003c/A\u003e\u0026nbsp;(formerly \u003cA href=\"https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/search/whats-new#new-service-name\" target=\"_self\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\"\u003eAzure Cognitive Search\u003c/A\u003e) and offer additional capabilities that complement and build on vector search to deliver markedly improved relevance.\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cP\u003eIn this blog post, we share the results of experiments conducted on Azure AI Search and present a quantitative basis to support the use of \u003cSTRONG\u003ehybrid retrieval + semantic ranking\u003c/STRONG\u003e as the most effective approach for improved relevance out-of–the-box. This is especially true for Generative AI scenarios where applications use the RAG pattern, though these conclusions apply to many general search use cases as well.\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cH2 id=\"community-3929167-toc-hId-589690501\"\u003e1. The Technology Behind Azure AI Search\u003c/H2\u003e\n\u003cP\u003eThe query stack in Azure AI Search follows a pattern that’s often used in sophisticated search systems, where there are two main layers of execution: \u003cSTRONG\u003eretrieval\u003c/STRONG\u003e and \u003cSTRONG\u003eranking\u003c/STRONG\u003e.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u003cSTRONG\u003eRetrieval\u003c/STRONG\u003e – Often called L1, the goal of this step is to quickly find all the documents from the index that satisfy the search criteria -possibly across millions or billions of documents. These are scored to pick the top few (typically in the order of 50) to return to the user or to feed to the next layer. Azure AI Search supports (3) different L1 modes:\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cUL\u003e\n\u003cLI\u003e\u003cA href=\"https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/search/search-lucene-query-architecture\" target=\"_self\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\"\u003e\u003cSTRONG\u003eKeyword\u003c/STRONG\u003e\u003c/A\u003e: Uses traditional full-text search methods – content is broken into terms through language-specific text analysis, inverted indexes are created for fast retrieval, and the BM25 probabilistic model is used for scoring.\u003c/LI\u003e\n\u003cLI\u003e\u003cA href=\"https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/search/vector-search-overview\" target=\"_self\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\"\u003e\u003cSTRONG\u003eVector\u003c/STRONG\u003e\u003c/A\u003e: Documents are converted from text to vector representations using an embedding model. Retrieval is performed by generating a query embedding and finding the documents whose vectors are closest to the query’s.\u0026nbsp; We used Azure Open AI \u003cA href=\"https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/ai-services/openai/concepts/models#embeddings-models-1\" target=\"_self\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\"\u003etext-embedding-ada-002\u003c/A\u003e (Ada-002) embeddings and cosine similarity for all our tests in this post.\u003c/LI\u003e\n\u003cLI\u003e\u003cSTRONG\u003e\u003cA href=\"https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/search/vector-search-ranking#hybrid-search\" target=\"_self\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\"\u003eHybrid\u003c/A\u003e\u003c/STRONG\u003e: Performs both keyword and vector retrieval and applies a fusion step to select the best results from each technique. Azure AI Search currently uses \u003cA href=\"https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/search/vector-search-ranking#reciprocal-rank-fusion-rrf-for-hybrid-queries\" target=\"_self\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\"\u003eReciprocal Rank Fusion\u003c/A\u003e (RRF) to produce a single result set.\u003c/LI\u003e\n\u003c/UL\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u003cSTRONG\u003eRanking\u003c/STRONG\u003e – also called L2, takes a subset of the top L1 results and computes higher quality relevance scores to reorder the result set. The L2 can improve the L1's ranking because it applies more computational power to each result. The L2 ranker can only reorder what the L1 already found – if the L1 missed an ideal document, the L2 can't fix that. L2 ranking is critical for RAG applications to make sure the best results are in the top positions.\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cUL\u003e\n\u003cLI\u003e\u003cA href=\"https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/search/semantic-search-overview\" target=\"_self\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\"\u003e\u003cSTRONG\u003eSemantic ranking\u003c/STRONG\u003e\u003c/A\u003e is performed by Azure AI Search's L2 ranker which utilizes multi-lingual, deep learning models adapted from Microsoft Bing. The Semantic ranker can rank the top 50 results from the L1.\u003c/LI\u003e\n\u003c/UL\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cH2 id=\"community-3929167-toc-hId--1217763962\"\u003e2. Hybrid Retrieval + Semantic Ranking yields the best grounding results for Generative AI Applications\u003c/H2\u003e\n\u003cP\u003eGenerative AI applications need to be grounded by content retrieved from indexes that contain the knowledge necessary for relevant responses. If irrelevant content is passed to the LLM, it works counter to this objective, and requires the model to filter extraneous information.\u0026nbsp; This can reduce the quality of generated responses and increase latency and operating costs.\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cP\u003eWe performed tests on representative customer indexes as well as popular academic benchmarks to test the quality of content retrieval results. Across the board, the most effective retrieval engine for most scenarios is achieved by:\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cOL\u003e\n\u003cLI\u003echunking long form content,\u003c/LI\u003e\n\u003cLI\u003eusing hybrid retrieval (combining keyword and vector search), and\u003c/LI\u003e\n\u003cLI\u003eenabling semantic ranking\u0026nbsp;\u003c/LI\u003e\n\u003c/OL\u003e\n\u003cDIV 1\u003c/STRONG\u003e: Retrieval comparison using Azure AI Search in various retrieval modes on customer and academic benchmarks. See \u003cA href=\"#community-3929167-generated\" target=\"_self\" rel=\"noopener nofollow noreferrer\"\u003e§6.1 How we generated the numbers in this post\u003c/A\u003e and \u003cA href=\"#community-3929167-searchconfiguration\" target=\"_self\" rel=\"noopener nofollow noreferrer\"\u003e§6.2 Search and Dataset configuration for Table 1\u003c/A\u003e for the setup and measurement details.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/EM\u003e\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cH2 id=\"community-3929167-toc-hId-1269748871\"\u003e3. Hybrid Retrieval brings out the best of Keyword and Vector Search\u003c/H2\u003e\n\u003cP\u003eKeyword and vector retrieval tackle search from different perspectives, which yield complementary capabilities. Vector retrieval semantically matches queries to passages with similar meanings. This is powerful because embeddings are less sensitive to misspellings, synonyms, and phrasing differences and can even work in cross lingual scenarios. Keyword search is useful because it prioritizes matching specific, important words that might be diluted in an embedding.\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cP\u003eUser search can take many forms. Hybrid retrieval consistently brings out the best from both retrieval methods across query types. With the most effective L1, the L2 ranking step can significantly improve the quality of results in the top positions.\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cDIV 2\u003c/STRONG\u003e: NDCG@3 comparison across query types and search configurations. See \u003cA href=\"#community-3929167-querytype\" target=\"_self\" rel=\"noopener nofollow noreferrer\"\u003e§6.3 Query Type definitions for Table 2\u003c/A\u003e for a more detailed description of each query type. All vector retrieval modes used the same document chunks (512 token chunks w/25% overlap with Ada-002 embedding model over customer query/document benchmark). Sentence boundaries were preserved in all cases.\u003c/EM\u003e\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cH2 id=\"community-3929167-toc-hId--537705592\"\u003e4. Your Document Chunking strategy matters\u003c/H2\u003e\n\u003cP\u003eChunking solves 3 problems for Generative AI applications:\u0026nbsp;\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cOL\u003e\n\u003cLI\u003eSplitting long documents into limited-length passages allows multiple retrieved documents to be passed to the LLM within its context window limit.\u003c/LI\u003e\n\u003cLI\u003eChunking provides a mechanism for the most relevant passages of a given document to be ranked first.\u003c/LI\u003e\n\u003cLI\u003eVector search has a per-model limit to how much content can be embedded into each vector.\u003c/LI\u003e\n\u003c/OL\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cP\u003eEmbedding each chunk into its own vector keeps the input within the embedding model’s token limit and enables the entire document to be searchable in an ANN search index without truncation. Most deep embedding models have a limit of 512 tokens. Ada-002 has a limit of 8,192 tokens. Moderate length documents can have tens of thousands of tokens. The benefit of chunking is particularly strong when the documents are very long or the answers to queries are found later in the document:\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cDIV class=\"styles_lia-table-wrapper__h6Xo9 styles_table-responsive__MW0lN\"\u003e\n\u003cTABLE whose answer is in long documents\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003cTD class=\"lia-align-right\" style=\"text-align : right;\"\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e28.2\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003cTD class=\"lia-align-right\" style=\"text-align : right;\"\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u003cSTRONG\u003e45.7 \u003c/STRONG\u003e\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003c/TR\u003e\n\u003cTR\u003e\n\u003cTD\u003e\n\u003cP\u003eQueries whose answer is deep into a document\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003cTD class=\"lia-align-right\" style=\"text-align : right;\"\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e28.7\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003cTD class=\"lia-align-right\" style=\"text-align : right;\"\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u003cSTRONG\u003e51.4 \u003c/STRONG\u003e\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003c/TR\u003e\n\u003c/TBODY\u003e\n\u003c/TABLE\u003e\n\u003c/DIV\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u003cEM\u003e\u003cSTRONG\u003eTable 3\u003c/STRONG\u003e: Recall@50 comparison using (1) a single vector to represent each document (first 4096 tokens of each document were vectorized and the rest were truncated) vs (2) chunking each document into 512 token chunks w/25% overlap with Ada-002 embedding model over customer query/document benchmark. Sentence boundaries were preserved in all cases. Metric computed with vector retrieval only (no Semantic ranking).\u0026nbsp;\u003c/EM\u003e\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cP\u003eAnother key consideration is that embedding models must compress all the semantic content of a passage into a limited number of floating-point numbers (e.g. Ada-002 uses 1,536 dimensions). If developers encode a long passage with multiple topics into a single vector, important nuance can get lost. Our analysis shows that using large chunks reduces retrieval performance.\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cDIV class=\"styles_lia-table-wrapper__h6Xo9 styles_table-responsive__MW0lN\"\u003e\n\u003cTABLE class=\"lia-indent-margin-left-30px\" border=\"1\"\u003e\n\u003cTBODY\u003e\n\u003cTR\u003e\n\u003cTD\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u003cSTRONG\u003eRetrieval Configuration \u003c/STRONG\u003e\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003cTD class=\"lia-align-right\" style=\"text-align : right;\"\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u003cSTRONG\u003eRecall@50\u003c/STRONG\u003e\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003c/TR\u003e\n\u003cTR\u003e\n\u003cTD\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e512 input tokens per vector\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003cTD class=\"lia-align-right\" style=\"text-align : right;\"\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u003cSTRONG\u003e42.4\u003c/STRONG\u003e\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003c/TR\u003e\n\u003cTR\u003e\n\u003cTD\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e1024 input tokens per vector\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003cTD class=\"lia-align-right\" style=\"text-align : right;\"\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e37.5\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003c/TR\u003e\n\u003cTR\u003e\n\u003cTD\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e4096 input tokens per vector\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003cTD class=\"lia-align-right\" style=\"text-align : right;\"\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e36.4\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003c/TR\u003e\n\u003cTR\u003e\n\u003cTD\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e8191 input tokens per vector\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003cTD class=\"lia-align-right\" style=\"text-align : right;\"\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e34.9\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003c/TR\u003e\n\u003c/TBODY\u003e\n\u003c/TABLE\u003e\n\u003c/DIV\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u003cEM\u003e\u003cSTRONG\u003eTable 4\u003c/STRONG\u003e: Recall@50 comparison of different chunk sizes with Ada-002 embedding model over customer query/document benchmark. Sentence boundaries were preserved in all cases. Metric computed with vector retrieval only (no Semantic ranking).\u003c/EM\u003e\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cP\u003eThere are many ways developers can build the input for each vector. For example, they can overlap each chunk so there is shared context between them, or they can add in the document title or key topics into each vector to give more context. A strategy of terminating vectors at natural sentence and paragraph breaks is both simple and effective.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cDIV class=\"styles_lia-table-wrapper__h6Xo9 styles_table-responsive__MW0lN\"\u003e\n\u003cTABLE class=\"lia-indent-margin-left-30px\" border=\"1\"\u003e\n\u003cTBODY\u003e\n\u003cTR\u003e\n\u003cTD\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u003cSTRONG\u003eChunk boundary strategy\u003c/STRONG\u003e\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003cTD class=\"lia-align-right\" style=\"text-align : right;\"\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u003cSTRONG\u003eRecall@50\u003c/STRONG\u003e\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003c/TR\u003e\n\u003cTR\u003e\n\u003cTD\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e512 tokens, break at token boundary\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003cTD class=\"lia-align-right\" style=\"text-align : right;\"\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e40.9\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003c/TR\u003e\n\u003cTR\u003e\n\u003cTD\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e512 tokens, preserve sentence boundaries\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003cTD class=\"lia-align-right\" style=\"text-align : right;\"\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e42.4\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003c/TR\u003e\n\u003cTR\u003e\n\u003cTD\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e512 tokens with 10% overlapping chunks\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003cTD class=\"lia-align-right\" style=\"text-align : right;\"\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e43.1\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003c/TR\u003e\n\u003cTR\u003e\n\u003cTD\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e512 tokens with 25% overlapping chunks\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003cTD class=\"lia-align-right\" style=\"text-align : right;\"\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u003cSTRONG\u003e43.9\u003c/STRONG\u003e\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003c/TR\u003e\n\u003c/TBODY\u003e\n\u003c/TABLE\u003e\n\u003c/DIV\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u003cEM\u003e\u003cSTRONG\u003eTable 5\u003c/STRONG\u003e: Recall@50 comparison of different chunk boundary strategies using 512 tokens with Ada002 embedding model over customer query/document benchmark. Metric computed with vector retrieval only (no semantic ranking).\u003c/EM\u003e\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cH2 id=\"community-3929167-toc-hId-1949807241\"\u003e5. Semantic Ranking Puts the Best Results at the Top\u003c/H2\u003e\n\u003cP\u003eGenerative AI scenarios typically use the top 3 to 5 results as their grounding context to prioritize the most important results. AI Search applications work best with a calibrated relevance score that can be used to filter out low quality results.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cP\u003eThe semantic ranker runs the query and documents text simultaneously though transformer models that utilize the cross-attention mechanism to produce a ranker score. The query and document chunk score is calibrated to a range that is consistent across all indexes and queries. A score of 0 represents a very irrelevant chunk, and a score of 4 represents an excellent one. In the chart below, Hybrid + Semantic ranking finds the best content for the LLM at each result set size.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cspan class=\"lia-media-object lia-media-is-center lia-media-size-default\" data-image-alt=\"\"\u003e\u003cbutton class=\"lia-media-unstyled-btn\" type=\"button\" aria-haspopup=\"true\" aria-label=\"Enlarge Image\"\u003e\u003cimg src=\"https://techcommunity.microsoft.com/t5/s/gxcuf89792/images/bS0zOTI5MTY3LWtaeVV3Sw?image-dimensions=750x418\u0026amp;revision=43\" width=\"750\" height=\"418\" alt=\"\" /\u003e\u003c/button\u003e\u003c/span\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u003cEM\u003e\u003cSTRONG\u003eChart 1\u003c/STRONG\u003e: Percentage of queries where high-quality chunks are found in the top 1 to 5 results, compared across search configurations. All retrieval modes used the same set of customer query/document benchmark. Document chunks were 512 tokens with 25% overlap.\u0026nbsp; Vector and hybrid retrieval used Ada-002 embeddings.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/EM\u003e\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cP\u003eIn conclusion, the results of the above experiments on real-world and benchmark datasets lead us to recommend the combined strategies of chunked content, hybrid search, and semantic ranking. To test these findings against your users’ questions and datasets, please try the resources linked below to get started:\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cUL\u003e\n\u003cLI\u003e\u003cA href=\"https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/search/\" target=\"_self\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\"\u003eAzure AI Search documentation | Microsoft Learn\u003c/A\u003e\u003c/LI\u003e\n\u003cLI\u003e\u003cA href=\"https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/search/semantic-search-overview\" target=\"_self\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\"\u003eSemantic ranker (formerly semantic search) - Azure AI Search | Microsoft Learn\u003c/A\u003e\u003c/LI\u003e\n\u003c/UL\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cH2 id=\"community-3929167-toc-hId-142352778\"\u003e6. Appendix – How we ran these tests\u003c/H2\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cH4 id=\"community-3929167-generated\"\u003e6.1 How we generated the numbers in this post\u003c/H4\u003e\n\u003cP\u003eTo assess which retrieval systems and configurations performed the best, we followed best practices to generate comparable metrics. The high-level process was to replay a list of queries against several document indexes for each configuration and produce scores of how good the retrieval and ranking was.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cUL\u003e\n\u003cLI\u003e\u003cSTRONG\u003eDocuments\u003c/STRONG\u003e - We use a consistent set of documents sourced from either Azure customers (with their permission) or publicly available benchmarks.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/LI\u003e\n\u003cLI\u003e\u003cSTRONG\u003eQueries\u003c/STRONG\u003e – We used a combination of end user queries and/or queries generated by several different GPT prompts using random snippets from the document index as grounding.\u003c/LI\u003e\n\u003cLI\u003e\u003cSTRONG\u003eScoring\u003c/STRONG\u003e – We used benchmark-provided labels (and the official scoring library) for BEIR and other datasets. For customer datasets, we use a GPT prompt that was vetted against a library of high quality (internally reviewed) human ground-truth labels.\u003c/LI\u003e\n\u003c/UL\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cP\u003eWe used 3 metrics to determine our recommendations:\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cUL\u003e\n\u003cLI\u003e\u003cSTRONG\u003eNDCG@10\u003c/STRONG\u003e – NDCG is a common information retrieval metric that provides a score between 0 and 100 based on how well a retrieval system (1) found the best results and (2) put those results in the ideal order (i.e. a sorted list from the best document to the worst) for all the queries in a given query set. The @10 means that the top 10 documents were considered in the score calculation. We used this metric and the pool of available labels for public benchmarks to be consistent with previous runs. \u003cA href=\"https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Discounted_cumulative_gain#Normalized_DCG\" target=\"_self\" rel=\"nofollow noopener noreferrer\"\u003eNormalized Discounted Cumulative Gain (NDCG)\u003c/A\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/LI\u003e\n\u003cLI\u003e\u003cSTRONG\u003eNDCG@3\u003c/STRONG\u003e – The same NDCG metric but computed on the top 3 documents. We use @3 because we aim to get the most accurate results in the top (3) for generative AI scenarios. We score the top 50 documents because Azure AI Search’s semantic ranker works on the top 50 results.\u003c/LI\u003e\n\u003cLI\u003e\u003cSTRONG\u003eRecall@50\u003c/STRONG\u003e – We count the number of documents that our scoring prompt rates as high quality within the top 50 retrieved results and divide it by the number of known good documents for that query.\u003c/LI\u003e\n\u003c/UL\u003e\n\u003cH4 id=\"community-3929167-searchconfiguration\"\u003e6.2 Search and Dataset configuration for Table 1\u003c/H4\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u003cSTRONG\u003eSearch Configuration\u003c/STRONG\u003e\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cP\u003eFor this table of results, all documents were broken into 512 token chunks w/25% overlap.\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cUL\u003e\n\u003cLI\u003e\u003cSTRONG\u003eKeyword\u003c/STRONG\u003e: The full set of chunks were indexed as if each chunk was a full document. Searches were performed as usual with the keyword-based index (BM25 similarity) and we labeled the top 50.\u003c/LI\u003e\n\u003cLI\u003e\u003cSTRONG\u003eVector\u003c/STRONG\u003e: All the chunks were embedded using Ada-002 and an ANN index was built. Each query was also embedded with Ada-002 and searched using cosine similarity. The top 50 were labeled.\u003c/LI\u003e\n\u003cLI\u003e\u003cSTRONG\u003eHybrid\u003c/STRONG\u003e: The keyword index and vector index of the chunks were searched (taking the top 50 from each) and then the results were fused together using RRF. The top 50 documents (chunks) from RRF were labeled.\u003c/LI\u003e\n\u003cLI\u003e\u003cSTRONG\u003eHybrid + \u003cA href=\"https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/search/semantic-search-overview\" target=\"_self\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\"\u003esemantic ranking\u003c/A\u003e\u003c/STRONG\u003e: Queries were performed against the hybrid search configuration with semantic ranking enabled.\u003c/LI\u003e\n\u003c/UL\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u003cSTRONG\u003eDataset details\u003c/STRONG\u003e\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cUL\u003e\n\u003cLI\u003e\u003cSTRONG\u003eCustomer datasets\u003c/STRONG\u003e – retrieval benchmarks built from 4 different customer datasets spanned industries and document structures. All documents were imported from raw inputs (e.g. pptx, pdf, html) using Azure AI Search’s document ingestion pipeline. Queries for each dataset are a mixture of provided and GPT-generated queries. For ANN vector retrieval tests, all documents were chunked into 512 tokens with 25% overlap and embedded using Ada-002.\u003c/LI\u003e\n\u003cLI\u003e\u003cSTRONG\u003eBEIR\u003c/STRONG\u003e – Imported the following datasets from the official source (\u003cA href=\"https://github.com/beir-cellar/beir\" target=\"_self\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\"\u003ebeir-cellar/beir: A Heterogeneous Benchmark for Information Retrieval. Easy to use, evaluate your models across 15+ diverse IR datasets. (github.com)\u003c/A\u003e) and used the official labels and official scoring library to generate metrics. For ANN vector retrieval, each document was indexed using Ada-002 (in 99.9% of cases 1 vector was sufficient to represent the full document). Beir datasets used: nq, scidocs, touche2020, arguana, climate-fever, dbpedia, fever, hotpotqa, covid.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/LI\u003e\n\u003cLI\u003e\u003cSTRONG\u003eMiracl\u003c/STRONG\u003e – Imported directly from the official source (\u003cA href=\"https://github.com/project-miracl/miracl\" target=\"_self\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\"\u003eproject-miracl/miracl: A large-scale multilingual dataset for Information Retrieval. Thorough human-annotations across 18 diverse languages. (github.com)\u003c/A\u003e) and used official labels for scoring. Due to the size of the dataset the following simplifications were used: we sampled 250 queries per language and only documents with labels were vectorized and a combined multi-lingual index was built. The reported metric is the average across ar, bn, de, en, es, fa, fi, fr, hi, id, ja, ko, ru, sw, te, th, yo, zh languages.\u003c/LI\u003e\n\u003c/UL\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cH4 id=\"community-3929167-querytype\"\u003e6.3 Query Type definitions for Table 2\u003c/H4\u003e\n\u003cDIV class=\"styles_lia-table-wrapper__h6Xo9 styles_table-responsive__MW0lN\"\u003e\n\u003cTABLE border=\"1\"\u003e\n\u003cTBODY\u003e\n\u003cTR\u003e\n\u003cTD\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u003cSTRONG\u003eQuery type\u003c/STRONG\u003e\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003cTD\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u003cSTRONG\u003eExplanation\u003c/STRONG\u003e\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003cTD\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u003cSTRONG\u003eExample\u003c/STRONG\u003e\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003c/TR\u003e\n\u003cTR\u003e\n\u003cTD\u003e\n\u003cP\u003eConcept seeking queries\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003cTD\u003e\n\u003cP\u003eAbstract questions that require multiple sentences to answer\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003cTD\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e“Why should I use semantic search to rank results?”\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003c/TR\u003e\n\u003cTR\u003e\n\u003cTD\u003e\n\u003cP\u003eExact snippet search\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003cTD\u003e\n\u003cP\u003eLonger queries that are exact sub-strings from the original paragraph\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003cTD\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e“enables you to maximize the quality and value of your LLM investments most efficiently by feeding only relevant information”\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003c/TR\u003e\n\u003cTR\u003e\n\u003cTD\u003e\n\u003cP\u003eWeb search-like queries\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003cTD\u003e\n\u003cP\u003eShortened queries similar to those commonly entered into a search engine\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003cTD\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e“Best retrieval concept queries”\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003c/TR\u003e\n\u003cTR\u003e\n\u003cTD\u003e\n\u003cP\u003eLow query/doc term overlap\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003cTD\u003e\n\u003cP\u003eQueries where the answer uses different words and phrases from the question [which can be challenging for a retrieval engine to find]\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003cTD\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e“greatest technology for sorting” searching for a document that says: “Azure AI Search has the best models for ranking your content”\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003c/TR\u003e\n\u003cTR\u003e\n\u003cTD\u003e\n\u003cP\u003eFact seeking queries\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003cTD\u003e\n\u003cP\u003eQueries with a single, clear answer\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003cTD\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e“How many documents are semantically ranked”\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003c/TR\u003e\n\u003cTR\u003e\n\u003cTD\u003e\n\u003cP\u003eKeyword queries\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003cTD\u003e\n\u003cP\u003eShort queries that consist of only the important identifier words.\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003cTD\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e“semantic ranker”\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003c/TR\u003e\n\u003cTR\u003e\n\u003cTD\u003e\n\u003cP\u003eQueries with misspellings\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003cTD\u003e\n\u003cP\u003eQueries with typos, transpositions and common misspellings introduced\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003cTD\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e“Ho w mny documents are samantically r4nked”\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003c/TR\u003e\n\u003cTR\u003e\n\u003cTD\u003e\n\u003cP\u003eLong queries\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003cTD\u003e\n\u003cP\u003eQueries longer than 20 tokens\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003cTD\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e“This is a very long query that uses a lot of tokens in its composition and structure because it is verbose”\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003c/TR\u003e\n\u003cTR\u003e\n\u003cTD\u003e\n\u003cP\u003eMedium queries\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003cTD\u003e\n\u003cP\u003eBetween 5 and 20 tokens long\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003cTD\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e“This is a medium length query”\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003c/TR\u003e\n\u003cTR\u003e\n\u003cTD\u003e\n\u003cP\u003eShort queries\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003cTD\u003e\n\u003cP\u003eQueries shorter than 5 tokens\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003cTD\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e“Short query”.\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003c/TD\u003e\n\u003c/TR\u003e\n\u003c/TBODY\u003e\n\u003c/TABLE\u003e\n\u003c/DIV\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/P\u003e","body@stringLength":"30029","rawBody":"\u003cP\u003e\u003cSPAN data-teams=\"true\"\u003e\u003cEM\u003eNote: Since this blog's publication, the Azure AI Search team has released new learnings on RAG quality. \u003c/EM\u003e\u003cA href=\"https://techcommunity.microsoft.com/blog/azure-ai-services-blog/raising-the-bar-for-rag-excellence-query-rewriting-and-new-semantic-ranker/4302729\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\" aria-label=\"Link Check out the latest report\"\u003e\u003cEM\u003eCheck out the latest report\u003c/EM\u003e\u003c/A\u003e\u003cEM\u003e covering our evaluations on query pipeline performance, and the product improvements made based on these learnings.\u003c/EM\u003e\u003c/SPAN\u003e\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cP\u003eA common practice for implementing the retrieval step in retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) applications is to use vector search. This approach finds relevant passages using semantic similarity. We fully support this pattern in\u0026nbsp;\u003cA href=\"https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/products/ai-services/cognitive-search\" target=\"_self\"\u003eAzure AI Search\u003c/A\u003e\u0026nbsp;(formerly \u003cA href=\"https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/search/whats-new#new-service-name\" target=\"_self\"\u003eAzure Cognitive Search\u003c/A\u003e) and offer additional capabilities that complement and build on vector search to deliver markedly improved relevance.\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cP\u003eIn this blog post, we share the results of experiments conducted on Azure AI Search and present a quantitative basis to support the use of \u003cSTRONG\u003ehybrid retrieval + semantic ranking\u003c/STRONG\u003e as the most effective approach for improved relevance out-of–the-box. This is especially true for Generative AI scenarios where applications use the RAG pattern, though these conclusions apply to many general search use cases as well.\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cH2\u003e1. The Technology Behind Azure AI Search\u003c/H2\u003e\n\u003cP\u003eThe query stack in Azure AI Search follows a pattern that’s often used in sophisticated search systems, where there are two main layers of execution: \u003cSTRONG\u003eretrieval\u003c/STRONG\u003e and \u003cSTRONG\u003eranking\u003c/STRONG\u003e.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u003cSTRONG\u003eRetrieval\u003c/STRONG\u003e – Often called L1, the goal of this step is to quickly find all the documents from the index that satisfy the search criteria -possibly across millions or billions of documents. These are scored to pick the top few (typically in the order of 50) to return to the user or to feed to the next layer. Azure AI Search supports (3) different L1 modes:\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cUL\u003e\n\u003cLI\u003e\u003cA href=\"https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/search/search-lucene-query-architecture\" target=\"_self\"\u003e\u003cSTRONG\u003eKeyword\u003c/STRONG\u003e\u003c/A\u003e: Uses traditional full-text search methods – content is broken into terms through language-specific text analysis, inverted indexes are created for fast retrieval, and the BM25 probabilistic model is used for scoring.\u003c/LI\u003e\n\u003cLI\u003e\u003cA href=\"https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/search/vector-search-overview\" target=\"_self\"\u003e\u003cSTRONG\u003eVector\u003c/STRONG\u003e\u003c/A\u003e: Documents are converted from text to vector representations using an embedding model. Retrieval is performed by generating a query embedding and finding the documents whose vectors are closest to the query’s.\u0026nbsp; We used Azure Open AI \u003cA href=\"https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/ai-services/openai/concepts/models#embeddings-models-1\" target=\"_self\"\u003etext-embedding-ada-002\u003c/A\u003e (Ada-002) embeddings and cosine similarity for all our tests in this post.\u003c/LI\u003e\n\u003cLI\u003e\u003cSTRONG\u003e\u003cA href=\"https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/search/vector-search-ranking#hybrid-search\" target=\"_self\"\u003eHybrid\u003c/A\u003e\u003c/STRONG\u003e: Performs both keyword and vector retrieval and applies a fusion step to select the best results from each technique. Azure AI Search currently uses \u003cA href=\"https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/search/vector-search-ranking#reciprocal-rank-fusion-rrf-for-hybrid-queries\" target=\"_self\"\u003eReciprocal Rank Fusion\u003c/A\u003e (RRF) to produce a single result set.\u003c/LI\u003e\n\u003c/UL\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u003cSTRONG\u003eRanking\u003c/STRONG\u003e – also called L2, takes a subset of the top L1 results and computes higher quality relevance scores to reorder the result set. The L2 can improve the L1's ranking because it applies more computational power to each result. The L2 ranker can only reorder what the L1 already found – if the L1 missed an ideal document, the L2 can't fix that. L2 ranking is critical for RAG applications to make sure the best results are in the top positions.\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cUL\u003e\n\u003cLI\u003e\u003cA href=\"https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/search/semantic-search-overview\" target=\"_self\"\u003e\u003cSTRONG\u003eSemantic ranking\u003c/STRONG\u003e\u003c/A\u003e is performed by Azure AI Search's L2 ranker which utilizes multi-lingual, deep learning models adapted from Microsoft Bing. The Semantic ranker can rank the top 50 results from the L1.\u003c/LI\u003e\n\u003c/UL\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cH2\u003e2. Hybrid Retrieval + Semantic Ranking yields the best grounding results for Generative AI Applications\u003c/H2\u003e\n\u003cP\u003eGenerative AI applications need to be grounded by content retrieved from indexes that contain the knowledge necessary for relevant responses. If irrelevant content is passed to the LLM, it works counter to this objective, and requires the model to filter extraneous information.\u0026nbsp; This can reduce the quality of generated responses and increase latency and operating costs.\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cP\u003eWe performed tests on representative customer indexes as well as popular academic benchmarks to test the quality of content retrieval results. Across the board, the most effective retrieval engine for most scenarios is achieved by:\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cOL\u003e\n\u003cLI\u003echunking long form content,\u003c/LI\u003e\n\u003cLI\u003eusing hybrid retrieval (combining keyword and vector search), and\u003c/LI\u003e\n\u003cLI\u003eenabling semantic ranking\u0026nbsp;\u003c/LI\u003e\n\u003c/OL\u003e\n\u003cDIV 1\u003c/STRONG\u003e: Retrieval comparison using Azure AI Search in various retrieval modes on customer and academic benchmarks. See \u003cA href=\"#generated\" target=\"_self\" rel=\"noopener\"\u003e§6.1 How we generated the numbers in this post\u003c/A\u003e and \u003cA href=\"#searchconfiguration\" target=\"_self\" rel=\"noopener\"\u003e§6.2 Search and Dataset configuration for Table 1\u003c/A\u003e for the setup and measurement details.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/EM\u003e\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cH2\u003e3. Hybrid Retrieval brings out the best of Keyword and Vector Search\u003c/H2\u003e\n\u003cP\u003eKeyword and vector retrieval tackle search from different perspectives, which yield complementary capabilities. Vector retrieval semantically matches queries to passages with similar meanings. This is powerful because embeddings are less sensitive to misspellings, synonyms, and phrasing differences and can even work in cross lingual scenarios. Keyword search is useful because it prioritizes matching specific, important words that might be diluted in an embedding.\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cP\u003eUser search can take many forms. Hybrid retrieval consistently brings out the best from both retrieval methods across query types. With the most effective L1, the L2 ranking step can significantly improve the quality of results in the top positions.\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cP\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/P\u003e\n\u003cDIV
